NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
Fern Benally • Alberto L. Peshlakai • Jason E. Whiting • Daryl Seymore • Dawnafe Whitesinger
“We are Navajo County”
 
NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING AND AGENDA
Tuesday, May 26, 2026
NAVAJO COUNTY GOVERNMENTAL COMPLEX
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS' CHAMBERS
100 EAST CODE TALKERS DRIVE
HOLBROOK, AZ 86025
One or more supervisors may attend telephonically
Pursuant to A.R.S. §38-431.02(H), the public body will have physical access to the meeting place one (1) hour prior to the start of
the meeting. The Board may vote to meet in a closed executive session to discuss certain matters and for legal advice on any item
as indicated on the following agenda, pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03(A)(3). Items on the agenda may be considered out of order at
the Chairperson's discretion. A copy of the agenda background material provided to the Board, except items to be considered in the
executive session, is available for public inspection at the Navajo County website, www.navajocountyaz.gov 
WATCH THE MEETING LIVE AT: www.navajocountyaz.gov/660
All public comments will need to be made in person or in writing. Written comments will be received by the Clerk of the Board's
office twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board meeting, at leah.thomas@navajocountyaz.gov. 
Individuals seeking to address the Board during the Call to the Public or when an agenda item is called must fill out a
“Request to Speak” form and provide it to the Clerk of the Board of Supervisors before the meeting is called to order. Late
submissions will not be accepted. The form must indicate whether the item the person wishes to speak on is on the agenda.
 Members of the public present to speak to the Board on an agenda item must so indicate, and are asked to refrain from speaking
during the general Call to the Public.  Persons present to address agenda items will be called on during a public comment period by
the Board Chairman after the relevant agenda item is called. Individuals addressing the Board during the general Call to the Public
are limited to three minutes.  Individuals addressing the Board when an item is called are also limited to three minutes, however,
additional time may be allotted by the Chairman to address any specific questions asked by members of the Board.
Persons with disabilities who need accommodation to attend or participate in the meeting may contact the Clerk of the Board’s
office at least forty-eight (48) hours prior to the meeting, so accommodation can be arranged.
“NOTICE TO PARENTS AND LEGAL GUARDIANS: Parents and legal guardians have the right to consent before Navajo County makes a video or voice recording
of a minor child, pursuant to A.R.S. § 1-602(A)(9). The Navajo County Board of Supervisors' regular meetings are recorded and may be viewed on Navajo County’s
website and social media pages. If you permit your child to attend/participate in a televised Navajo County Board of Supervisors meeting, a recording will be made, and
your child’s picture may be posted on Navajo County’s social media pages. You may exercise your right not to consent by not allowing your child to attend/participate
in the meeting.”

agenda.

NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS’ MINUTES
Tuesday, May 12, 2026
Present: Fern Benally, Supervisor District I; Alberto Peshlakai, Supervisor
District II; Jason Whiting, Supervisor District III; Daryl Seymore,
Chairman; Dawnafe Whitesinger, Vice-Chair 
Staff
Attendance: 
Bryan Layton, County Manager; Brad Carlyon, County Attorney; Leah
Thomas, Clerk of the Board 
9:00 a.m. Call To Order: Invocation and Pledge of Allegiance was led by Chairman
Seymore. 
The National Anthem was sung by Holbrook Schools Rocking Roadrunner 4th and
5th Grade Choir
         
CALL TO THE PUBLIC: 
Chairman Seymore opened the Call to the Public and requested that speakers avoid
repeating comments already presented to the Board, whether by themselves or by
previous speakers.
Liz Olmstead greeted the Board stating that she lives at 7093 Tumbleweed Trail in
Linden/Show Low, AZ. Ms. Olmsted petitioned the Board on behalf of the people of
Navajo County for decisive action to protect groundwater, rural life, and the integrity of
county government. She expressed her view that the economic group, Real AZ had
been dissolved on October 17, 2024, and is continuing to illegally operate using county
staff and facilities, the county seal, and public resources.  Ms. Olmstead referred to ARS
10-3804, 10-3805, and 38-503 which she stated Real AZ is in violation of.  Ms. Olmsted
expressed concerns about Real AZ donors linked to the West Camp II windfarm and
perceived influence over policy changes and project approvals. Ms. Olmstead stated
that their petition notes the Board’s previous enactment of Resolutions 1 2025 and 17
2025, which they believe promote renewable energy development while weakening
existing protections, as well as the cancellation of all 2026 Planning and Zoning
Commission meetings, which they say has limited public input on utility-scale wind
proposals, particularly the proposed West Camp Wind Farm II. The petition argues that
such projects would require substantial groundwater for concrete foundations and for
dust suppression on new roadways in a region already experiencing aquifer stress and
declining spring flows, and that no independent water-use or groundwater studies have
been conducted. Ms. Olmstead further noted that the projects would qualify for an 80
percent property-tax reduction under ARS 42 14155(B), resulting in minimal long-term
revenue to the County while leaving environmental and infrastructure costs
unaddressed. Additional concerns she cited included potential impacts on rural
landscapes, property values, wildlife habitat, and residents’ quality of life, compounded
by the absence of baseline soil testing and transparent independent analysis. To
address these issues, the petition requests: an immediate moratorium on all new
utility-scale wind, solar, and battery-energy-storage approvals until the stated concerns
are resolved; the rescission of Resolutions 1 2025 and 17 2025 in their entirety; and the
severance of all ties with the dissolved Real AZ organization.
Donna Wastchak greeted the Board stating she is from Pinetop.  Ms. Wastchak
addressed the Board regarding statements made during the April 28th Board meeting
concerning the approval of the West Camp 2 Wind Farm Development Agreement. She
stated that it was claimed the Board was required to approve the agreement and
                                                                    1 of 10

<!-- page 7 -->
requested that the Board research that claim. Ms. Wastchak provided information on
Arizona Revised Statute (ARS) 11 1101, stating that the statute gives counties
discretion and authority within development agreements, including the ability to
negotiate protections such as independent water-use studies, environmental studies,
and requiring a Power Purchase Agreement prior to approval. She expressed concern
that renewable projects could be built without being used if such safeguards were not
required. Ms. Wastchak further stated that she believes Resolution 01-2025 and
Resolution 17-2025, weaken county ordinances and created a fast-track approval
process that removed key protections. She asked the Board to consider rescinding both
resolutions and to use ARS 11 1101 as a basis for strengthening future development
agreements. She also referenced ARS 11 833, stating that the statute provides the
Board authority to impose an immediate moratorium on new utility-scale renewable
energy projects until proper water, soil, and environmental studies are completed.
Ms. Wastchak asked the Board to listen to constituents who want stronger protections
included in development agreements and urged the Board to use the statutes to act in
public interest. She concluded by thanking the Board and provided copies of the statute.
Andrew Bushman introduced himself stating that he is from Joseph City and that he is
working to buy back his family farm one piece at a time. He said that Joseph City and
part of McLaws Road are part of a non-expansion district for irrigation dating back to the
1970’s, explaining that; that means that he cannot water more acres than what there
was at the time the non-expansion district was established and that puts a cap on his
business being able to expand. Mr. Bushman claimed that a data center will use
hundreds and thousands of gallons of water each day is frustrating when he does not
have access to water to improve his existing business.  Mr. Bushman outlined the
changes to the Joseph City Irrigation District and shared his frustrations with large
companies.  Mr. Bushman then turned his remaining time to Ms. Olmstead.
Liz Olmstead asked the Board to adopt the following conditions for renewable projects:
two-mile setbacks from residences, springs, and wells, a signed Power Purchase
Agreement, independent third-party water-use and groundwater studies, baseline soil
and water testing, and robust decommissioning, bonding, and site-restoration
guarantees. She also requested that the petition be placed on the agenda for the next
regularly scheduled Board meeting for public hearing and consideration. Ms. Olmsted
stated that residents urge the Board to prioritize the interests of Navajo County residents
over outside investors. 
Cheryl Piha greeted the Board and shared her concern that there is a lot of people who
will get hurt if the solar and wind farms progress. She stated that she has lived in Texas,
California, New York, Washington, Missouri, Florida, and now Arizona and has seen that
they do not work.  She stated that she has heard about corruption and that if things keep
being the same the country will be lost.  She asked the Board to remember that they
serve the people who elected them and to use their power wisely.
Belinda Quog greeted the Board stating that she is from Show Low, Arizona.  Ms. Quog
stated that the area is in a severe, multi-decade drought and that water supplies for
millions of people, agricultural needs, and ecosystems are affected and causing extreme
fire dangers. Ms. Quog stated that many wells in Navajo and Apache Counties have
dried up, forcing residents to truck in water or dig deeper wells.  Ms. Quog stated that
there is no water to waste on large-scale industrial projects that have no long-term
benefits to the community.  Ms. Quog then referred to the state’s 80% tax break for
renewable developments, stating that the tax break shows favoritism to corporations
over citizens who pay full property taxes. She expressed concern that these projects
have no water-recycling capability and use significant amounts of water for both
construction and ongoing maintenance. Ms. Quog stated her belief that the decisions
                                                                    2 of 10

<!-- page 8 -->
made by the Board of Supervisors, the County Attorney, the County Manager, Real, AZ,
Rose Law Firm, foreign corporations, and local property owners who have signed project
leases demonstrate a lack of foresight regarding water conservation, environmental
preservation, and community welfare. She noted that, in her view, these actions could
lead to serious consequences for local communities. She further stated that members of
the public are alarmed by what they perceive as private, closed-door meetings related
to these projects. Ms. Quog expressed concern that, without effective
water-management planning, the region could face significant water shortages. For
these reasons, she requested that the Board impose an immediate moratorium on all
utility-scale wind, solar, and battery energy storage project approvals until additional
studies are completed. She urged the Board to consider the potential consequences of
its decisions.
Casey Murph thanked the Board for the opportunity to speak, he stated that he is the 4th
generation of his family to be born and 5th generation of his family to live in Perkins
Valley, AZ. He stated that he is a cattle rancher that will be affected by the installation of
a utility scale solar installation on state grazing lands. Mr. Murph said that he used his
retirement to purchase his land from his relatives, pointing out that the price of beef has
risen because in the past 40 years there have been 1 million independent cattle
ranchers pushed out of business.  Mr. Murph said that utility solar uses land and pushes
people like himself out of business.
Mr. Robert Black thanked the Board for the opportunity to speak.  He addressed the
Board regarding renewable energy projects. He expressed disappointment with prior
comments in which the Board stated that there was nothing that could be done if
applicants met Planning and Zoning requirements. Mr. Black stated his belief that the
Board does have options and presented a petition containing over 500 signatures
opposing wind and solar projects, noting that signatures were collected within a week
and continue to increase. Mr. Black reported concerns about an oily, sticky substance
observed in cattle drinkers and on grasslands west of McLaws Road, which he believes
to be associated with nearby renewable energy projects. He stated that he is having the
substance tested and has retained a sample. He expressed concern that residents are
being negatively affected and urged the Board not to dismiss public concerns. Mr. Black
stated that public outcry is growing and indicated that residents will continue attending
Board meetings until these issues are addressed. He expressed the view that it is
the Board’s responsibility to protect citizens and work with the State if needed. He urged
the Board to take action, stating that renewable projects are harming local communities
and that residents are not willing to live with the current conditions. Mr. Black concluded
by asking the Board to “figure it out,” reiterating that he believes it is the Board’s
responsibility to do so.

COUNTY ATTORNEY:
a. Presentation to Catherine Doyle,  Navajo County FAC Director, on receiving the
Guara Award. 
Roxanne Pergeson explained that Catherine Doyle, the Director of the Family
Advocacy Center, was recently recognized for a state award, the Guerra Award,
given through the Arizona Coalition to end sexual and domestic violence. She
explained that the award honors individuals or organizations that demonstrate
bold and nontraditional advocacy in the movement to end sexual and domestic
violence. The award recognizes those who think creatively and act
courageously, whether by building unique connections with survivors, shaping
innovative policies, or introducing new approaches that transform how support
and advocacy are delivered.  Ms. Pergeson then read the nomination letter and
shared that Ms. Doyle has been a valuable asset to the Family Advocacy
Center. She stated that she has seen Ms. Doyle advance the Center in
meaningful ways and that she has demonstrated strong vision, fearlessness,
and a tireless commitment to serving children. Ms. Pergeson added that Ms.
Doyle brings great passion to her work and that she is honored to have her as
part of the team and as the recipient of the award.
Catherine then addressed the Board, stating it was good to see them again. She
thanked Roxanne, the County Attorney’s Office, and Navajo County for their
trust in her work with local children. She expressed hope that prevention and
                                                                    4 of 10

<!-- page 10 -->
outreach efforts will continue and emphasized her commitment to supporting
children.
Chairman Seymore thanked Ms. Doyle for her work and stated that it is an
honor for both her and the County to have received the award. He commented
that any time the County can help children or individuals in abusive situations, it
is important to have someone who can provide calm, support, and assistance in
seeking justice. He thanked Ms. Doyle for her dedication and expressed
appreciation for the work of her department.
Brad Carylon explained that Catherine had presented to the Board
approximately six weeks earlier regarding the programs she has implemented at
the Family Advocacy Center. He emphasized that, given the size of Navajo
County and its limited resources, the work requires creative solutions. He stated
that Catherine’s drive and commitment to helping young victims have resulted in
effective programs that other rural entities are beginning to emulate.  He stated
that the County is fortunate to have Catherine, and that the victims served in
Navajo County benefit greatly from the programs she has created
 
SUPERINTENDENT OF SCHOOLS: 
a. Presentation of Navajo County "What America Means to Me" Essay Contest
Winners. 
Rachael Lewis greeted the Board and expressed appreciation for their
partnership in hosting the essay contest in celebration of 250 years of America.
She stated that patriotism is taught and nurtured by dedicated teachers, parents,
and leaders who help instill a love of country. Ms. Lewis introduced the essay
contest winners:
Harper Wittren, a first grade student from Whipple Ranch Elementary; Abby
Greer, a fourth grade student from St. Anthony’s Catholic School; Adah
Guevara, a seventh grade student from Show Low Jr. High (not present); Jadyn
Melser, a sophomore at Snowflake High School.
Chairman Seymore said that all the essays were great, congratulated the
winners, and read Harper Wittren’s essay.
Ms. Lewis shared that there were nearly 100 entries.
 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY:
a. Consideration of a new three year contract with Microsoft. 
Ken Dewitt greeted the Board and presented a request for approval of a new
Microsoft contract, noting that it is a continuation of the Microsoft products the
County has used for many years. He explained that the County enters into three
year agreements and that the contract has been reviewed. The agreement
provides access to products such as Word, Excel, PowerPoint, email services, a
limited version of Copilot, server and PC operating systems, cloud storage, and
other related tools. Mr. Dewitt stated that the contract cost has increased by
approximately $2,000 per year, which he noted is significantly lower than typical
increases. He explained that the department has focused on selecting only the
products that are necessary and actively used. He recommended approval of the
contract.
Chairman Seymore thanked Mr. Dewitt for his work on the project.
Supervisor Benally made a motion to approve a new three-year contract with
Microsoft, motion seconded by Supervisor Whiting. Vote unanimous approving
the motion.

PUBLIC WORKS:
a. Presentation recognizing the Public Works Fleet Maintenance Shop Team for
the Cold Mix Hot Plate Box Attachment conversion innovation, with a brief
overview of the Arizona LTAP program. 
Will Flake greeted the Board and stated that ADOT has a program called the
Arizona Local Technical Assistance Program (LTAP) that has a sub-program
called build a better mousetrap where they recognize innovative people in the
industry.  Mr. Flake explained that Mr. Mundle submitted a Navajo County Public
Works innovation to the program.  Mr. Flake then introduced Josh Luchtel from
LTAP.
A presentation was shown.
Josh Luchtel greeted the Board and explained that he serves as the statewide
heavy-equipment trainer, working across all regions of the state. He stated that
he observes many innovative practices, including agencies repurposing older
equipment to reduce costs. He noted that Navajo County’s maintenance crews
also consistently work to save money by repurposing equipment and avoiding
new purchases unless they are necessary. He commended staff for bringing
forward their ideas and solutions. Mr. Luchtel explained that the LTAP Program
receives federal funding, which supports training opportunities for counties,
councils of governments, and MPOs. He stated that these partners are eligible
for the program’s training and on-demand classes, which help elevate
employees’ skills.
He reported that the program provides a wide range of courses, from
corporate-style training and first aid/CPR to heavy-equipment instruction. He
noted that many agencies, including Navajo County, use the program as a
pathway for employees to advance through the Roads Scholar system. Mr.
Luchtel highlighted an example of innovation by the County’s maintenance team,
explaining that staff repurposed an old chip spreader box to create a heated
storage solution for materials that typically become unusable during winter
months. He stated that this modification allows crews to use materials
year-round and reflects the ingenuity of County employees.  He then presented
Navajo County employees, Russ McCray and Gabe Carrizales with the award
stating that the innovations are submitted on a nationwide and statewide level
and LTAP likes to highlight the projects that bring the most amount of good.
Chairman Seymore expressed appreciation to the team for their ingenuity.
 
COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE: 
Supervisor Whiting reported that he will be continuing conversations with several utility
partners regarding energy development in the region. He stated that he has a meeting
scheduled with APS to discuss the Cholla Power Plant and its long-term outlook for
northern Arizona. He also noted that he has been invited to participate in a discussion
with SRP in the coming week regarding nuclear options and future energy opportunities.
Supervisor Whiting expressed appreciation for these organizations’ continued
engagement with Navajo County as they work collaboratively on matters related to
economic development, infrastructure, reliability, jobs, and long-term regional planning. 
Supervisor Benally reported that she attended the County Supervisors Association
(CSA) meetings during the month. She also attended an NDOT/HDOT/Navajo Nation
group meeting on May 27, where she met with representatives to discuss roadway
conditions and potential improvements. She shared that she participated in the Western
Interstate Region (WIR) Conference, where she attended board meetings and sessions
                                                                    6 of 10

<!-- page 12 -->
Interstate Region (WIR) Conference, where she attended board meetings and sessions
on topics such as water conservation, tourism, disaster response, recovery training, and
post-fire recovery efforts in Lahaina. She viewed a private screening of Lahaina Rising,
a film about people who sought safety in the ocean during the fire event. She noted that
the film may be accessible on WIR . Supervisor Benally also reported that she
attended the Navajo Nation Division of Natural Resources Summit to learn more about
compliance, archaeology, biological considerations, and right-of-way processes. She
noted additional presentations by the Department of Minerals and Water Resources
regarding Navajo water rights and implementation plans. She stated that she continues
to attend her monthly Chapter meetings, quarterly Agency meetings, and Veterans
meetings. She noted that she missed graduation ceremonies for NPC and Pinon but
thanked colleagues who attended the Northland Pioneer College groundbreaking in
Kayenta, expressing appreciation for their willingness to make the trip to Kayenta. 
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger expressed her gratitude to all who contributed to the
success of the second annual Wildland Fire Preparedness Expo. She thanked the
community for their positive feedback and noted the value of providing residents with
resources to learn how to mitigate their properties and where to seek additional
information. She also thanked the County staff and representatives who attended the
White Mountain Apache Tribe’s inaugural ceremony for the newly elected Tribal
Chairman, Vice Chair, and Council Members. She emphasized the importance of
partnerships within Navajo County and across the state, noting that strong relationships
with tribal communities are essential to effective governance and regional
collaboration. 
Chairman Seymore noted that some of the items raised during Call to the Public will
return as future agenda items in June. He then acknowledged the end-of-school-year
activities occurring throughout the County. He wished all graduating students success
going forward and thanked the citizens in attendance at the meeting. 
 
ADJOURN:   10:00 a.m.
  
WORK SESSION: Pursuant to ARS § 38-431.02 and ARS § 38-431(3), notice is hereby
given to the members of the Navajo County Board of Supervisors and to the Public that
a quorum of the Board of Supervisors may be present for a work session immediately
following the Board of Supervisors Agenda in the Board Chambers where no official
action will be taken.
10:24 a.m. Chairman Seymore called the Work Session to order. 
 
a. Presentation and review of Public Works projects, including the Capital
Improvement Program and projects for the next fiscal year. 
Bryan Layton talked about how the county has invested in upgrading its GIS
mapping system, so residents can look up their streets, see who maintains
them, and when maintenance occurred, with access via a QR code on business
cards, which leads to the county website. He indicated that county roads are
those not maintained by federal, state, municipal, or tribal entities, totaling about
300 miles of paved and 350 miles of unpaved roads. He indicated the new
pavement management program, which replaced informal visual inspections with
a complete, measured inventory that allows data-driven, resource-efficient
decisions to keep the overall road network in the best possible condition.
Will Flake outlined the county’s approach to road preservation, keeping good
roads in good condition, versus maintenance and rehabilitation, fixing more
                                                                    7 of 10

<!-- page 13 -->
roads in good condition, versus maintenance and rehabilitation, fixing more
serious issues, covering both paved and gravel roads. He explains that a
pavement condition survey conducted in August 2023 rated all paved sections
with a Pavement Condition Index, showing an average Pavement Condition
Index (PCI) of 73 with over 80% of roads in good or better condition, giving the
county a clear baseline and roadmap for future work. He spoke about targeted
pavement preservation and rehabilitation projects in specific areas, grouped to
reduce cost and mobilization, including nearly two miles of rehabilitation on
seven roads that currently have a low average PCI of 46, indicating they
essentially need to be rebuilt once they reach that level of deterioration.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger inquired if the scale was from 1 to 100.
Mr. Flake indicated that the PCI is scored on a 0–100 scale.  He explained that
primitive gravel roads are graded at least every eight weeks, usually more often,
with non‑primitive gravel roads on a four‑week cycle and high‑traffic routes
targeted about every three weeks, all tracked on the county website by district and
date. He provided a brief overview of the five‑year Capital Improvement Program
covering engineering, flood control, highway maintenance, and fleet, updated
annually to keep a rolling plan of funded projects.
Supervisor Benally asked about the difference between slurry seal, chip seal and
resurfacing.
Mr. Flake explained that slurry seal is a thin oil‑and‑aggregate treatment used to fill
small cracks and protect pavement from water damage, especially when it freezes
in the winter, while chip seal is a similar preservation treatment usually applied
after slurry seal, adding small rock chips to the oil layer to create a more durable
protective surface. 
Supervisor Benally inquired about the tar that was put into the cracks. 
Mr. Flake indicated that it is called crack sealing, which is used as a preventative
maintenance treatment, noting that while the county does not use it extensively,
they do apply it in certain areas.
Chairman Seymore inquired about Borden Ranch Road, noting that a property
owner granted right‑of‑way when the road was widened, with a promise that
pavement would be extended to their properties and certain cattle guards
installed.  He indicated that the that paving has not yet occurred.
Mr. Flake stated he would talk with staff regarding the concern.  Mr. Flake explained
that CIP projects are reviewed annually, completed items are removed, and new
needs added, with top priority given to public safety and preserving existing roads
so they don’t deteriorate to the point of costly reconstruction, and he invited
ongoing board input on priorities across their districts.
Chairman Seymore shared a concern regarding whether the county is allocating
enough in capital improvements to at least maintain current overall road conditions
in the face of inflation and long‑term funding needs.
Mr. Flake indicated that they are working on gathering data and will provide more
information.  
Supervisor Peshlakai spoke about recent non‑reservation projects in his district
and asked how the county can partner with the City of Holbrook on improving the
road to the Holbrook Golf Course in Perkins Valley.  He inquired how needs are
identified with partner projects off reservation so he can be more engaged in those
projects.  
Mr. Flake indicated that the county has partnered with municipalities before
through IGA's and financial contributions to city‑led projects, confirming that such a
partnership approach is definitely possible.
                                                                    8 of 10

<!-- page 14 -->
Rochelle Lacapa outlined the framework for partnering on non‑county
transportation projects, which includes confirming the request fits within the
board‑approved budget and $100,000 procurement threshold, giving priority
to shovel‑ready projects with completed studies/designs and a clear scope and cost
from the lead agency, and ensuring the work is realistically achievable within fiscal
year procurement timelines.  She noted this framework is used consistently when
working with ADOT, NDOT, White Mountain Apache, and municipalities on
partnership projects.  
Nicole Reske reported on District One and District Two partnerships, noting
completed and in‑progress projects such as gravel improvements and pothole
patching on key Navajo Nation routes, a legacy road project finished in April, and
critical support for BIA by providing the only heavy equipment operator for the
Defiance area, while outlining forecasted projects based on community and chapter
priorities.
Mr. Flake highlighted partnership work in Districts 3 and 4, including ongoing Silver
Creek flood‑control cleaning with local partners, the planned Seventh Street
extension in Snowflake, the future Scott Ranch Road infrastructure expansion, and
early discussions with the City of Show Low about widening and reconstructing
Ellsworth Road.  
Mr. Layton explained that municipal partnership projects typically start when a city
identifies a road with both city and county impacts, such as Ellsworth Road,
approaches the county to explore a cost‑share, and then staff work through that
process.  He encouraged the Supervisors to talk with city managers to identify such
joint opportunities, noting he also meets regularly with city and county managers to
discuss shared issues and potential projects.
Chairman Seymore spoke about a long‑standing regional project to complete a
bridge and road connection near Home Depot, suggesting the county consider
redirecting its Scott Ranch Road partnership funds to help the City of Show Low
build the more attainable Wolfford Road bridge so at least one critical bridge
project can move forward rather than waiting indefinitely on state or federal
funding.
Mr. Flake indicated that the suggestion makes sense, noting the new bridge design is
already being worked on. 
Ms. Reske highlighted successful District 5 partnerships, including annual funding
for much‑needed streetlights in Whiteriver and a bus turnaround improvement
that now safely accommodates school buses, noting recent tribal requests for
striping on Industrial Road and a new guardrail project on BIA 12.
Ms. Lacapa spoke about the guardrail project, noting it required complex
coordination with both BIA and Gila County.  She used the BIA 12 guardrail project
to illustrate how complex multi‑jurisdictional partnerships can require
coordination with another county’s management and legal teams to clarify roles
and agreements, noting that working through this has created a template that will
make similar cross‑county tribal projects easier going forward.
Ms. Reske explained that successful tribal partnerships depend on aligning
priorities through regular meetings, using longer IGA's to fit tribal approval
timelines, consistently educating communities about what county partnership funds
can cover, and first confirming who owns each road and formally notifying that lead
agency, especially on complex, cross‑jurisdictional reservation routes.
Ms. Lacapa commended Ms. Reske for significantly expanding and strengthening
tribal transportation partnerships.  She likened coordinating with other agencies
to timing your jump into a moving jump rope, where joining too early can disrupt
                                                                    9 of 10

<!-- page 15 -->
their process, and said Nicole, Will, and Bryan have learned how to “jump in” at the
right moment, so the county helps support, rather than slows, partner projects.
Mr. Layton praised Ms. Reske’s relationship‑building with tribal partners.  He asked
if the Board had any feedback or direction on the CIP, data‑driven pavement
management, or partnership efforts, emphasizing that while staff now knows
exactly which roads are in the worst condition, there is still room for Board
discretion based on what Supervisors see and hear in their districts.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger spoke about how far the county has come in organizing
and prioritizing roadwork, praising the shift toward equity and fairness across
districts rather than popularity or politics, and commended the professional,
relationship‑based work with tribal communities, highlighting a recent direct
outreach from a tribal department director as a sign that Navajo County is viewed
as a reliable partner.
Chairman Seymore shared his appreciation for the collaborative work.
Mr. Layton provided a brief update on budget meetings with departments.
11:06 a.m. the Work Session adjourned. 
APPROVED:
Daryl Seymore, Chairman
Navajo County Board of Supervisors

8:46 a.m. Call To Order: Chairman Seymore called the meeting to order.  
         
1. NAVAJO COUNTY PUBLIC HEALTH SERVICES DISTRICT: Board of
Directors Session: 
Supervisor Peshlakai made a motion to enter into the Navajo County Public
Health Services District Board of Directors Session, motion seconded by
Vice-Chair Whitesinger.  Motion passed 4-0 with Supervisor Whiting being
absent.
8:47 a.m. Supervisor Jason Whiting is present virtually. 
 
a. Consideration of a Declaration of Emergency due to extreme fire danger and to
enact Section 6 of the Navajo County Ordinance 01-19 Emergency Fire
Restrictions, to enter into Stage 1 fire restrictions :
Catrina Jenkins reported that during Tuesday’s fire restrictions call, the group
agreed to recommend moving into Stage 1 fire restrictions because conditions
are worsening. She indicated that a declaration of emergency is being
requested so that the county can initiate fire restrictions under Navajo County
Ordinance 01-19, Section 6.  Ms. Jenkins read the proposed Declaration of
Emergency.  She stated that they will distribute information about the fire
restrictions as soon as the other jurisdictions in the region—including the Forest
Service and state land—have approved their proclamations and ordinances, and
that they will work to get this information out as quickly as possible once all
approvals are in place.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to approve a Declaration of
Emergency due to extreme fire danger and to enact Section 6 of the Navajo
County Ordinance 01-19 Emergency Fire Restrictions, to enter into Stage 1 fire
restrictions, motion seconded by Supervisor Benally.  Vote unanimous
approving the motion. 
Vice-Chair Whitesinger made a motion to return to regular session, motion
seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote unanimous approving the motion.
Chairman Seymore thanked Ms. Jenkins for all she does.  
 
2. ADJOURN: 8:51 a.m. meeting adjourned.

for Juvenile Detention Services

The purpose of this IGA is for the provision of juvenile detention (“Services”) for up to 5 of Sender’s
juvenile detainees (Sender juvenile(s)) transferred by Sender to Host Facility. Sender acknowledges and
agrees that Host’s policies and practices may not necessarily coincide with those of Sender and that Host
and all inmates therein are managed and administered according to Host’s policies and practices and will
remain so for the duration of this IGA. Juveniles are those individuals under the age of eighteen years.

with other juveniles in a juvenile facility pursuant to applicable law.

Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 1 of 11 
 
 
INTERGOVERNMENTAL AGREEMENT  
- BY AND BETWEEN - 
NAVAJO COUNTY,   
AND THE SUPERIOR COURTS OF THE STATE OF ARIZONA IN AND FOR NAVAJO 
COUNTY,  
AND GILA COUNTY,   
AND THE SUPERIOR COURTS OF THE STATE OF ARIZONA IN AND FOR GILA COUNTY 
- FOR - 
JUVENILE DETENTION SERVICES  
 
THIS INTERGOVERNMENTAL AGREEMENT dated this   day of                     , 20  
(“IGA”), is made by and between the Sender and the Host, as set forth herein, for the detention of juveniles 
under the supervision of Sending Party. The parties to this IGA include Navajo County, a political 
subdivision of the State of Arizona (“Host County”), and the Superior Court of Arizona in and for Navajo 
County, on behalf of the Navajo Coun ty Juvenile Detention Center ( “ Host Facility”) (Host County and 
Host Facility may be collectively referred to as “Host”) —and— Gila County (“ Sending Authority”), and 
the Superior Court of Arizona in and for Gila County, on behalf of the Gila County Probation Department 
(“Sending Department”) (Sending County and Sending Department may be collectively referred to as 
“Sender”). The parties to this agreement may also be referred to individually as “Party” or collectively as 
“Parties” hereinafter. 
 
RECITALS 
WHEREAS, the Parties are authorized and responsible to provide for the detention of certain 
juveniles alleged to be delinquent or children who are incorrigible pursuant to A.R.S. §§ 8-305 and 8-301 
et seq. and the Parties desire to utilize available resources in an efficient and mutually beneficial manner 
according to applicable law; and  
WHEREAS, Navajo County maintains a juvenile detention center (Facility) where such juveniles 
may be detained—which Facility is separate and apart from any adult detention center in which adults are 
confined; and 
WHEREAS, the Presiding Judge of the Juvenile Division of the Superior Court in Navajo County 
supervises the Facility pursuant to A.R.S. § 8-306; and  
WHEREAS, A.R.S. §§ 11-952(J) and 11-951 et seq. authorize the Parties to enter into this IGA.  
 
AGREEMENT 
NOW, THEREFORE,  the Parties hereto, in consideration of the stipulations, covenants and 
agreements hereinafter set forth, do hereby agree as follows: 
 
1. PURPOSE AND INTENT 
The purpose of this IGA is for the provision of juvenile detention (“Services”) for up to 5 of  Sender’s 
juvenile detainees (Sender juvenile(s)) transferred by Sender to Host Facility. Sender acknowledges 
and agrees that Host’s policies and practices may not necessarily coincide with those of Sender and that 
Host and all inmates therein are managed and administered according to Host’s policies and practices 
and will remain so for the duration of this IGA. Juveniles are those individuals under the age of eighteen 
years. Sender acknowledges and agrees that Host will not accept juveniles: who are charged with 
nothing more than incorrigible offense(s); who are under the age of eight years; or detainees that cannot 
April 2614th

<!-- page 25 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 2 of 11 
 
 
be detained with other juveniles in a juvenile facility pursuant to applicable law.  
 
2. EFFECTIVE DATE AND DURATION 
A. Initial Term. Upon execution and signature below, this IGA shall become effective on the   
day of      , 20 , and shall remain in effect for one (1) year 
thereafter unless otherwise terminated or renewed as provided in this IGA.  
B. Termination. Either Party may terminate this IGA, with or without cause, by providing thirty (30) 
days’ advance written notice of termination to the other Party as set forth in Section 10.A. Each 
Party agrees to return any and all equipment and/or materials in its control or possession, if any, to 
the owner-Party no later than thirty (30) days after termination.  
C. Renewal. By mutual written agreement of the Parties, this IGA may be renewed for no more than 
nine (9) additional one-year terms (Subsequent Term(s)) pursuant to A.R.S. § 11-952(J).   
3. COSTS AND FEES. The Parties agree that a per diem rate per juvenile will be charged, and are to be 
paid by Sender under Section 4.B.ii below.   
4. MUTUAL OBLIGATIONS 
A. Under this IGA Host agrees to: 
i. Provide detention services (“Services”) for Sender juveniles in Host Facility according 
to applicable policies, practices, standards and requirements.  
Services provided shall include, but are not to be limited to, housing, food, 
clothing, normal hygiene, and other routine services and care, including routine 
medical and mental health care, education, recreation, and visitation. All Services will 
be provided in compliance with applicable laws, ordinances, state and federal standards 
and practices.  
All juveniles referred to Host by Sender will be treated according to the same rules 
and regulations applied to other detainees in the custody of the Host Facility. 
ii. Send billing invoices to Sender on a monthly basis per Section 7 below. 
iii. Manage the day-to-day supervision, operations and services for Sender juveniles. 
iv. Provide all staff, labor and services necessary for the Facility’s day-to-day use and 
operation and maintain and provide all staff, labor, materials, and routine in-house 
services and bear all routine costs associated with the Facility at no additional cost to 
Sender. 
v. Arrange for non-routine medical, mental health, and/or educational services by 
juveniles referred by Sender and detained at the Facility. These non-routine services 
include, but are not limited to, hospitalization, ambulance, psychiatric assessments, 
psych-ed evaluation, and medications.  
The Parties acknowledge and agree that non-routine services will result in 
additional expense(s) which are the sole responsibility of Sender.  
To the extent permitted by time, Host shall contact Sender to discuss any non-
routine services that arise. The Parties agree that such discussion shall not unduly delay 
any need for emergency services, including, but not limited to emergency medical 
attention. Host shall have the sole discretion and authority to determine whether a 
situation is an emergency or becomes an emergency while awaiting Sender’s 
concurrence under Section 4.B.vii and 4.D below and Sender shall defer to the Host’s 
1st day of May 26

<!-- page 26 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 3 of 11 
 
 
assessment of the situation and determination regarding the emergency / non-
emergency nature of the situation.  
vi. To the extent permitted by time, Host shall contact Sender prior to any non-routine 
services that arise and in order to discuss and concur in writing regarding said 
service(s) with Sender.  
vii. In the event of any emergency situation(s) and/or service(s) involving Sender 
juvenile(s), Host will notify Sender of within 24 hours of occurrence. 
viii. Provide twenty-four hours advance notice when Sender will need to retrieve a juvenile. 
ix. Observe and administer any records exchanged under this IGA pursuant to applicable 
law. 
B. Under this IGA Sender agrees to: 
i. Promptly contact Host Intake Unit at (928) 205-8395, prior to transporting a juvenile 
to the Facility for admission. Sender will also provide Host with: 
1. Applicable Court Order and/or Referral and Affidavit to detain the juvenile; and  
2. Any available records or information requested by Host including but not limited 
to, information regarding the juvenile's family history, behavioral issues, medical, 
mental health, psychological evaluations, school, and/or social history for 
admission and monitoring purposes. 
ii. Pay a fee of $225.00 per day per Sender juvenile detained.  
1. Pay an additional fee of $50.00 per day for each day a Sender juvenile is 
detained for more than 90 days.  
For the purposes of this subsection, ‘day’ means the juvenile is incarcerated in the 
Facility for more than two (2) hours within a calendar day. The standard daily fee may 
not be increased without the written agreement of the Parties. 
iii. Pay any and all Additional Expense(s) incurred under or arising from this IGA and 
reimburse Host for any and all Additional Expense(s) incurred by Host under this IGA. 
iv. Pay each invoice received no later than thirty (30) days after receiving the invoice per 
Section 7. 
v. Provide transportation to and from the Facility: when Sender juveniles are booked into 
and released from the Facility; when appearances are required at court hearings; and 
when medical, dental, or other appointments for any such juvenile are scheduled 
outside the Holbrook area. When available, Host staff may assist on transports within 
the Holbrook area. 
vi. Sender will not deliver to the Facility any juveniles under the influence of controlled 
substances, or experiencing serious medical or mental health concerns, including self-
harm behavior, without first obtaining a medical release from an appropriate medical 
and/or mental health professional or hospital. 
vii. Provide any records necessitated by the purposes of this IGA. Such records may 
include, but are not necessarily limited to, juvenile penological and discipline records, 
criminal background and history records, medical records, and mental health records. 
viii. Remain responsible for the transportation of juveniles to and from the Host facility. 
Notwithstanding this, in the event of a medical emergency or other appropriate 
circumstance Host may transport a Sender juvenile from the Host Facility to the

<!-- page 27 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 4 of 11 
 
 
appropriate destination and Sender agrees that such instances may result in Additional 
Expense(s). 
ix. Ensure that no juvenile shall be held at Facility for more than twenty-four (24) hours 
unless a petition alleging delinquent conduct or a criminal complaint has been filed 
and a copy provided to the Host Intake unit. No juvenile shall be held longer than 
twenty-four hours after the filing of a petition unless so ordered by the court after a 
hearing.  
If a hearing is not held within twenty-four hours after the filing of the petition, 
Sender shall ensure that the juvenile shall be released from Facility to a parent, 
guardian, custodian or other responsible person. {NOTE: Juvenile Court requires 24 
hours petition filed when detained and a hearing 24 hours after petition is filed.}   If 
no parent, guardian, custodian or other responsible person can be located, Facility shall 
release the juvenile to the Department of Child Safety and Host shall notify Sending 
County Juvenile Court of the release. 
x. Observe and administer any records exchanged under this IGA pursuant to applicable 
law. 
xi. Upon notice from Host, promptly retrieve each Sender juvenile from the Host Facility 
not later than 24 hours after such notice from Host.   
C. Facility Capacity. The Parties acknowledge and agree that housing Sender juveniles will not be 
available if/when the population of the portion(s) of the Facility in operation at the time of referral 
is at capacity. If the Facility is at or near capacity, Host will work with Sender to determine which 
juveniles under the supervision of Sender should be released in the event the need arises for 
additional detention of juveniles at the Facility. 
D. Written Concurrence for Non-Routine Services. In the event of non-routine services the Parties 
agree to discuss said services and create a written concurrence regarding the services in advance. 
The Parties acknowledge that any emergency circumstances that arise may, in turn, make it 
unreasonable or unfeasible to discuss and/or create a written concurrence in advance. 
E. Additional Expenses . Additional Expense(s) include any and all costs associated with: 
transportation not expressly provided for in this IGA that are attributable to Sender juveniles; non-
routine services and/or materials required by Sender juveniles; and Sender juvenile medical and/or 
mental-health care and treatment not covered by the daily inmate fee referenced above. Medical 
and/or mental-health care and treatment not covered by the daily inmate fee include: prescription 
medication(s), hospital visits or any other medical/mental-health services requiring outside 
facilities, services or providers, and/or catastrophic medical or mental-health events.  
F. Remote Appearance Technology . To the maximum extent possible, and according to the 
mandates of the Court, the Parties intend to utilize available technology for the remote 
appearance/attendance of juveniles in hearings, proceedings, meetings, appointments, and 
evaluations when appropriate and available. Such means/methods may include audio/video 
conferencing technology and/or telephonic co mmunication. The purpose of doing so is to 
maximum the health and safety of all facility occupants and to maximize the efficient use of 
available time, staff and resources, subject to any requisite court approval or, when applicable, the 
professional discretion of involved physician(s), other psychiatric or medical professional(s), or 
legal counsel.

<!-- page 28 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 5 of 11 
 
 
G. Juvenile Legal Counsel. Host is not responsible for any legal representation required or requested 
by Sender juveniles. The provision of any such legal counsel and/or any arrangements therefore 
remain the responsibility of Sender.  
H. Sender Release. Sender shall notify Host Intake Unit at (928) 205-8395 when a Sender juvenile 
is to be released from the Facility to the custody of Sender. Any such release made at the request 
of Sender shall be at the sole discretion of the Sender and shall, with prior verbal notice confirmed 
by e-mail, be performed promptly and without undue delay by Host, and shall not require an order 
of a court. The Facility shall only release the detained juvenile upon notice from a specifically 
identified person the Sender has advised, in writing, is authorized to request release of a detained 
juvenile. The Facility shall only release the detained juvenile to a specifically identified person that 
Sender has advised, in writing, is authorized to take custody of the juvenile. 
I. Facility Acceptance or Rejection of Juvenile . Host retains final and absolute right to refuse 
acceptance or request removal of any Sender juvenile exhibiting violent or disruptive behavior, or 
of any juvenile found to have a medical condition that requires medical care beyond the scope of 
the Facility health provider. In the case of a juve nile already in Facility custody, Host shall notify 
Sender and request such removals, and shall allow Sender reasonable time to make alternative 
arrangements for the juvenile.  
J. Open Communications . Host and Sender shall maintain open communications between each 
Party’s designated point of contact [“POC”] (listed in Section 10.A) to ensure the agreed upon 
facilities and services are provided and maintained  throughout the term of th is IGA. Parties shall 
maintain open communication regarding needs arising out of the IGA. 
K. PREA. The Prison Rape Elimination Act (PREA) 34 U.S.C. §§ 30301—30309 and 28 C.F.R. §§ 
115.11—115.501 was established to address the elimination and prevention of sexual assault and 
sexual harassment within correctional systems and detention facilities. The Parties will comply 
with all standards. The Navajo County Sheriff’s Department will be assigned to investigate all 
incidents of sexual assault allegedly occurring within the Facility. The Parties agree to disclose any 
knowledge of sexual abuse or sexual harassment that a Sender juvenile may have encountered, 
whether as the perpetrator or the victim. 
L. Emergency Evacuation. In the event of any emergency requiring evacuation of the Facility, Host 
shall evacuate the juvenile in the same manner and with the same safeguards as other juveniles in 
the Facility. Host shall verbally notify Sender and confirm by e-mail or fax, within four (4) hours 
of such evacuation. 
M. Force Majeure. The Parties are not liable to each other if an occurrence of force majeure prevents 
its performance under the contract. If either party is delayed at any time in the progress of its 
performance under the contract by an occurrence of force majeure, the delayed party shall notify 
the other no later than the following working day after the occurrence, or as soon as it could 
reasonably have been expected to recognize that the occurrence had effect in cases where the effects 
were not readily apparent. In any event, the notice must make specific reference to this paragraph 
specifying the causes of the delay in the notice and, if the effects of the occurrence are on-going, 
provide an initial notification and thereafter the delayed party shall provide regular updates until 
such time as the effects are fully known. To the ex tent it is able, the delayed party shall cause the 
delay to cease promptly and notify the other party when it has done so.  
N. Public Health Crises. In the event of a public health crisis or the like (i.e. COVID 19 pandemic), 
Sender acknowledges and agrees that Facility is authorized to take all reasonable measures, as 
determined in the sole discretion of Facility, to protect the health and welfare of Facility occupants.

<!-- page 29 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 6 of 11 
 
 
Such measures may include, but are not limited to, stopping all non-essential ingress and/or egress 
to and from the Facility which may result in the discontinuation of programs and/or practices that 
pose a high risk of introducing and/or transmitting infection to or between Facility occupants. 
Additionally, Facility reserves the right to deny entry of a juvenile referred to Facility where, as 
determined in the sole discretion of Facility, the public interest in detention does not outweigh the 
risk of introducing infection into the Facility. Further, at the request of Host, Sender may be 
required to arrange for an initial quarantine detention of any such juvenile before the juvenile may 
be later transferred into Facility. 
5. SUPERVISION 
Each Party shall have sole supervisory authority over that party’s personnel, operations, services and 
materials. Each Party agrees that it will be solely responsible for and will assume sole liability for its 
officer’s acts or omissions of any kind, while performing any service or activity under this IGA. Each 
Party shall remain responsible for its employees' salaries and employee-related benefits, discipline and 
similar matters and shall be solely responsible for its employee's civil wrongs, and each employee shall 
be deemed to be performing regular duties for the primary employer Party while engaged in services 
and activities under this IGA. For the purposes of Workers' Compensation, the Party employing such 
employee shall be solely liable for the payment of Worker's Compensation benefits payable as the result 
of the employee's participation in services and activities under this IGA. 
6. EQUIPMENT AND MATERIALS 
The Parties agree to avoid using the other Party’s materials and/or equipment for purposes not directly 
associated with the purpose and intent of this IGA without the prior express written consent from the 
Party to whom the equipment and/or materials belong. However, this provision shall not be construed 
to prohibit any use of materials or equipment of another Party that is merely nominal and incidental, or 
on an emergency basis. 
7. BILLING/INVOICING 
Billing, invoicing and payment between the Parties arising out of this IGA shall be administered as 
follows: 
A. Host shall bill Sender on a monthly basis and send an itemized invoice promptly at the end of each 
month. 
B. Sender shall send payment to Host within thirty (30) days of receiving the invoice. 
C. All billing and payment correspondence shall be sent to the Party contacts provided in Section 
10.A. 
8. INSURANCE 
Each Party acknowledges and affirms that it has appr opriate and adequate insurance coverage for its 
official operations, duties and activities, and that it will maintain such coverage for the duration of this 
IGA.  
9. INDEMNIFICATION AND COOPERATIVE DEFENSE 
A. To the maximum extent permitted by law, the Parties agree to defend, indemnify and hold one 
harmless their elected officials, agents and employees, from and against any and all claims, actions, 
suits or proceedings of any kind brought against said parties because of any injury or damage 
received or sustained by person, persons, or property arising out of or resulting from the services

<!-- page 30 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 7 of 11 
 
 
performed under this Agreement.  The Host shall be indemnified against all expenses judgements, 
penalties, fines and amounts paid in settlement actually and reasonably incurred by them, or on 
their behalf, in connection with any such proceeding or any claim, issue or matter therein, arising 
from a juvenile, if the Host acted in good faith and in a manner the Host reasonably believed to be 
in or not opposed to the best interests of the Sender or the Sender juvenile.  
B. In the event that a claim, arising from or relating to the activities provided in this IGA, is made 
against any Party for acts or omissions of any of its employees or officers, it is the intent of the 
Parties to cooperate fully in the defense of said claim or claims and to cause their insurers to do 
likewise, to the extent practicable. 
C. The obligations under this section shall survive the termination of this IGA. 
10. GENERAL 
A. NOTICES: Except as otherwise provided in this IGA, all notices to the other Party required under 
this IGA shall be in writing and sent to the following personnel:

Notices under this Section shall be deemed completed and effective on the date delivered, if given 
by facsimile, personal delivery, email or overnight express delivery service, or four days after the 
date of deposit in the mail if sent through the United States Mail 
B. MODIFICATION: This IGA shall not be modified or extended except by a mutually signed 
written agreement. 
C. RELATIONSHIP OF THE PARTIES:  Each Party shall act in its individual capacity and not as 
an agent, employee, partner, joint venturer, associate, or any other representative capacity of the 
other party.  Each Party shall be solely and entirely responsible for its acts or acts of its agents and 
employees during the performance of this IGA.   This IGA shall not be construed to imply authority 
to perform any tasks, or accept any responsibility, not expressly set forth herein.  This IGA shall 
be strictly construed against the creation of a duty or  responsibility  unless  the  intention  to  do  
so  is  clearly and unambiguously  set forth herein.  Nothing contained in this IGA confers any right 
to any person or entity not a party to this IGA. 
D. WAIVER OF TERMS AND CONDITIONS: The failure of either Party to insist in any one or 
more instances on performance of any of the terms or conditions of this IGA or to exercise any 
right or privilege contained herein shall not be considered as thereafter waiving such terms,

<!-- page 31 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 8 of 11 
 
 
conditions, rights or privileges, and they shall remain in full force and effect. 
E. GOVERNING LAW AND VENUE: The terms and conditions of this IGA shall be governed by 
and interpreted in accordance with the laws of the State of Arizona. 
F. INUREMENT, NONASSIGNMENT, SUBCONTRACTING: All of the terms, covenants and 
conditions of this IGA shall be binding upon, and shall inure to the benefit of, each Party and the 
successors and assigns of each Party. The Parties shall not assign nor sub-contract their rights, 
duties, or obligations under this IGA without the prior written consent of the other Parties 
G. ENTIRE AGREEMENT:  This IGA represents the entire agreement between  the Parties and 
supersedes all prior negotiations, representations or agreements, either expressed or implied, 
written or oral. It is mutually understood and agreed that no alteration or variation of the terms and 
conditions of this IGA shall be valid unless made in writing and signed by the Parties. 
H. SEVERABILITY: If any part, term or provision of this IGA shall be held illegal, unenforceable 
or in conflict with any law, the validity of the remaining portions and provisions hereof shall not 
be affected. 
I. CONFLICTS OF INTEREST:  To the extent applicable, the provisions of A.R.S. § 38-511 
relating to cancellation of contracts due to conflicts of interest shall apply to this IGA. 
J. OTHER DUTIES IMPOSED BY LAW: Nothing in this IGA shall be construed as relieving the 
involved public agencies of any obligation or responsibility imposed on it by law. 
K. COMPLIANCE WITH CIVIL RIGHTS: To the extent applicable, the Parties agree to comply 
with A.R.S. Title 41, Chapter 9 (Civil Rights),  Arizona Executive Order 2009-09 and any other 
federal or state laws relating to equal opportunity and non-discrimination, including the Americans 
with Disabilities Act. 
L. E-VERIFY, RECORDS AND AUDITS:  To the extent applicable: Under A.R.S. § 41-4401, the 
Parties and their respective subcontractors warrant compliance with all federal immigration laws 
and regulations that relate to their employees and compliance with the E-verify requirements under 
A.R.S. § 23-214(A).   The Parties' or a subcontractor's breach of the above-mentioned warranty 
shall be deemed a material breach of the IGA and may result in the termination of the IGA by either 
party under the terms of this IGA.  The Parties each retain the legal right to randomly inspect the 
papers and records of each other Party and each other Party' subcontractors who work under this 
IGA to ensure that the other party and its subcontractors are complying with the above-mentioned 
warranty. The Parties warrant to keep their respective papers and records open for random 
inspection during normal business hours by each other Party. The Parties and their respective 
subcontractors shall cooperate with each other Party's random inspections including granting the 
inspecting Party entry rights onto their respective properties to perform the random inspections and 
waiving their respective rights to keep such papers and records confidential. The Parties agree to 
comply with the records retention requirements of A.R.S. § 35-214 
M. INTERPARTY DISPUTE RESOLUTION: If a dispute between the Parties arises out of or relates 
to this IGA, and if the dispute cannot be settled through negotiation within sixty (60) days, the Parties 
agree to resolve all disputes arising out the or relating to this Agreement through arbitration, after 
exhausting applicable administrative review, to the extent required by A.R.S. § 12-1518 except as 
may be required by other applicable statutes..  
N. AVAILABILITY OF FUNDS: In accordance with A.R.S. § 35-154, every payment obligation of 
each party under this Agreement is conditioned upon the availability of funds appropriated or 
allocated for the payment of such obligation.  If funds are not allocated and available for the

<!-- page 32 -->
Navajo Juvenile Detention IGA - Page 9 of 11 
 
 
continuance of this Agreement, this agreement may be terminated by either party at the end of the 
period for which funds are available.  No liability shall accrue to either party in the event this 
provision is exercised, and the parties shall not be obligated or liable for any future payments or 
for any damages as a result of termination under this paragraph.  
O. WORKER’S COMPENSATION:    To the extent applicable, each Party shall comply with the 
notice of A.R.S. § 23-1022(E). For purposes of A. R.S. § 23-1022, each Party shall be considered 
the primary employer of all personnel currently or hereafter employed by that Party, irrespective 
of the operations of protocol in place, and said Party shall have the sole responsibility for the 
payment of Worker’s Compensation benefits or other fringe benefits of said employees. 
P. COMPLIANCE WITH LAWS AND POLICIES:    To the extent applicable, the Parties shall 
comply with all federal, state and local laws, rules, regulations, standards and Executive Orders, 
without limitation to those designated within this IGA.  The laws and regulations of the State of 
Arizona shall govern the rights of the Parties, the performance of this IGA and any disputes 
hereunder. Furthermore, the Parties agree to abide by each Party’s policies to the extent appropriate 
and required or permitted by law. 
Q. NO JOINT VENTURE:   It is not intended by this IGA to, and nothing contained in this IGA 
shall, be construed to, create any partnership, joint venture or employment relationship between the 
Parties or create any employer-employee relationship between the Parties’ employees. Neither 
Party shall be liable for any debts, accounts, obligations or other liabilities whatsoever of the other 
Party, including, but without limitation, the other Party's obligation to withhold Social Security and 
income taxes for itself or any of its employees. 
R. NO THIRD PARTY BENEFICIARIES:    Nothing in this IGA is intended to create duties or 
obligations to or rights in third parties not Parties to this IGA or affect the legal liability of either 
Party to the IGA by imposing any standard of care with respect to the maintenance of public 
facilities different from the standard of care imposed by law. 
S. COUNTERPARTS. This IGA may be executed in any number of counterparts, each of which 
shall be deemed a duplicate original and all of which when taken together shall constitute one and 
the same document. Counterparts are effective and binding when this IGA has been executed by 
all of the Parties 
T. HEADINGS: The section headings throughout this IGA shall not be used in the construction or 
interpretation hereof as they have no substantive effect and are for convenience only.
